It implements the arc of a modern course, from tabular temporal-difference learning through linear function approximation to deep value-based and policy-gradient methods, with every algorithm written from first principles so that its update rule is visible in a few lines of code. All agents share one interface, so learners can compare methods directly across stages and trace a deep reinforcement learning update from the temporal-difference error to the weight change. The library supports teaching and assessment in which students implement, modify and explain update rules, rather than only produce working code.
  </details>

- **[SPT-Flow: One Integration, Many Futures in Continuous-Time World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41273)**  `arXiv:2610.41273`  
  _Mingyang Lyu, Yinqian Sun, Gongce Cao, Yuhao Zhang, Lu Jia, Qian Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous-time world models generate a latent trajectory through a single ODE integration, but adding stochastic conditioning does not ensure that the model uses it. In our teacher-forced baseline, posterior means vary by only about 1% of the prior scale, and matched and swapped latents yield indistinguishable reconstruction PSNR at the reported precision. We study a supervision-shortcut hypothesis: ground-truth intermediate states can reduce the need for trajectory-associated latent information. We introduce SPT-Flow, which samples one latent per trajectory and matches a free-running ODE rollout to demonstration states at multiple readout times. Re-anchoring experiments support the proposed mechanism. On LIBERO, with both models trained for 30k optimizer steps, SPT-Flow improves best-of-10 PSNR over our retrained ODEWorld baseline by 2.0 and 2.4 dB at the mid-trajectory and final frames and reduces mean LPIPS by 23%, with comparable per-sample inference cost. The rollout control recovers most of the accuracy gain; learned latent conditioning adds diversity and best-of-K gains while reducing mean sample accuracy relative to this control. The diagnostics support trajectory-associated latent utilization; semantic mode coverage under matched conditions in the robotic setting remains unverified.
  </details>

- **[Corroborative Learning: Mutual Enhancement through Task-Response Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41222)**  `arXiv:2610.41222`  
  _Zexing Zhang, Jichao Li, Tianyang Lei, Yiyin Zhang, Kewei Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In pattern analysis, models trained independently for the same task often develop complementary capabilities through differences in architecture, data, and optimization. Exploiting complementarity while retaining independent deployment is central to collaborative learning. Most methods establish transfer by reducing differences in predictions, relations, or representations between target and collaborator, although these differences also underlie complementarity. This creates a structural trade-off between reducing differences for knowledge exchange and preserving them for complementarity. We therefore propose Corroborative Learning (CL). Each model forms task-driven responses, with cross-group support from its collaborator. Source-excluded reconstruction of response increments from collaborator representations separates fitting and evaluation across disjoint groups and selectively constrains directions by cross-group reconstructability. The process requires no direct output or feature matching or additional inference dependency. Furthermore, with linear reconstruction and fixed collaborator representations, the loss equals a positive semidefinite directional regularizer, constraining less reconstructable directions more strongly. Signal and noise conditions characterize when directional shrinkage reduces response-estimation error. Across six language tasks, 24 models, and 36 pairs, CL improves over capacity-matched independent adaptation on every task, with positive gains across most directed model combinations. Pairwise comparisons show broad advantages over transfer and mutual-learning methods and different gains from complementarity across collaboration rules. Low-label language and vision results support cross-domain applicability. Task-relevant differences can support mutual enhancement without eliminating disagreement.
  </details>

- **[A Positional Realizability Theory of Length Generalization in Single-Head Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41218)**  `arXiv:2610.41218`  
  _Jiasong Han, Yunkai Chai, Jintao Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Why does length generalization (LG) depend so sharply on the positional encoding (PE)? Relative schemes extrapolate fixed offsets, absolute schemes fix positions, and neither handles growing offsets. We give a realizability theory for a deliberately controlled abstraction, a single positional-only attention head, that derives this well-documented empirical pattern and says precisely where it holds and breaks. The device is a simulation transfer operator $T_L$ that re-evaluates a selected length-$L_0$-realizing positional kernel on the longer grid, with a scalar $\alpha_L \in [0,1]$ read off before any training from $(\Phi, t_n)$ alone. Here $\alpha_L$ serves as the computable witness of that taxonomy's derivation, not as a stronger empirical predictor. Our results are a relative-head deep-row characterization for kernels with a strict unique global maximizer (Theorem 2), an absolute/relative duality that derives the pattern (Proposition 4), a learned-absolute underdetermination result, and a static expressivity bound; a sufficiency theorem (Theorem 1) bridges $\alpha_L$ to LG, and the general deterministic case is a precise open conjecture (correcting a tempting but invalid "outside-colspan" argument). Experiments confirm the structure: in the controlled single-head regime $\alpha_L$ exactly separates the taxonomy's generalizing and non-generalizing (PE, task) cells (2625 trained-model measurements; stable at vocab 64/d=128). Together, these results turn the received PE taxonomy into a positional-kernel transfer theorem, and identify the content and depth mechanisms that escape it. The theory concerns the positional channel alone: tasks whose targets depend on token content, including the standard length-generalization suite such as addition and sorting, fall outside Theorem 2.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Scarcity: When Encoder Adaptation Is Worthwhile](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41213)**  `arXiv:2610.41213`  
  _Zexing Zhang, Jichao Li, Tianyang Lei, Yiyin Zhang, Kewei Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-tailed recognition often learns a shared representation before correcting the classifier for class imbalance. Once the classifier has been rebalanced, however, final accuracy does not reveal whether further encoder adaptation remains worthwhile. Standard imbalance benchmarks obscure this question because they jointly vary the number of distinct examples available to rare classes and the frequency with which those examples shape the representation. We introduce a controlled evaluation framework that separates unique support from representation allocation and estimates the incremental value of encoder updates through matched classifier-only and full-network continuations from a common corrected model. The framework combines stage-specific controls, multiple representation and adaptation objectives, whole-distribution metrics, and hierarchical inference over class assignments, support reconstructions, and optimization runs. Across the controlled study, the same low-support regime moves from one in which classifier-only training is adequate to one in which encoder adaptation yields a practically meaningful benefit solely as representation allocation becomes more imbalanced. The transition persists across classifier corrections, representation objectives, normalization and augmentation controls, and ImageNet-scale evaluation, while preserving frequent-class accuracy. Under the prespecified one-percentage-point operating margin, the two regimes are separated by simultaneous confidence bounds. These results show that benchmark construction can alter a post-correction training decision and that support size alone does not characterize the remaining value of representation adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Role-Aware Server Optimization for Multimodal Federated Learning with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41193)**  `arXiv:2610.41193`  
  _Liuyin Shi, Wenbo He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal federated learning (MFL) trains a shared model by aggregating client updates without exchanging raw data. Under missing modalities, clients adapt active modality encoders and a shared prediction block (fusion and classifier) to different input subsets. This raises two server-side questions: which clients should contribute to each component, and how should their aggregates be incorporated across rounds? We observe that an absent-modality encoder can still adapt through missing-input handling. Availability- based aggregation can therefore omit its update while retaining jointly learned prediction-block updates. Contributor selection also shapes server momentum, a weighted history of aggregated updates. We observe that prediction-block momentum can improve or reduce performance depending on the encoder update policy. We therefore propose Role-Aware Server Optimization (RASO), which coordinates contributor selection with role-specific server updates. RASO selects contributors through prediction dependence and inclusion in local optimization, retains encoder momentum, and synchronizes the shared prediction block to its current aggregate. Experiments across datasets and fusion architectures demonstrate the effectiveness of RASO under missing modalities.
  </details>

- **[CAP: Learning to Optimize from Sparse Observations of Related Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41173)**  `arXiv:2610.41173`  
  _Lin Fu, Siwei Fu, Dong Ni_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In experiment-driven black-box optimization, each evaluation can consume materials, equipment time, and other resources, limiting the number of experiments available for a new task. Records from completed related optimization campaigns could help, but learning a sequential query policy remains challenging when task-specific experience consists of only a few sparse histories. We propose CAP (Candidate Acquisition Policy), which models shared structure and task-specific variation in fixed historical records to sample interactive practice functions. On these functions, CAP learns a feedback-conditioned query policy aimed at final optimization performance under a fixed evaluation budget. At deployment, its parameters remain fixed while each new observation informs the next query. Across five synthetic task families spanning 6 and 20 dimensions and two engineering simulation benchmarks, BOPTEST and SU2, CAP attains the lowest median final-budget value among the reported methods on four synthetic families and both engineering benchmarks. These results indicate that sparse records from completed related tasks can support adaptive query decisions on new tasks, making more effective use of limited, costly experimental evaluations.
  </details>

- **[TalentRL: Strategy-Guided Reinforcement Learning for Hierarchical Skill Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41143)**  `arXiv:2610.41143`  
  _Huancheng Chen, Xiaodi Sun, Zhaoqiong Huang, Michelle Chang, Jingwen Lu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural-language skills enable large language model (LLM) agents to reuse procedures across tasks, yet most systems retrieve individual skills from a flat SkillBank without explicitly modeling the high-level strategy that governs their selection and use. We introduce TalentRL, a strategy-guided reinforcement learning framework for hierarchical routing over procedural memory. Coarse-grained talents represent recurring strategies for skill use, while fine-grained skills provide executable procedures. For each task, the policy model selects a talent that guides execution and softly prioritizes its associated skills during similarity-based retrieval. However, since task outcomes depend jointly on talent selection, retrieved skill context, and downstream execution, terminal rewards alone cannot isolate the contribution of the routing decision. TalentRL therefore trains talent routing with reward contrasts between rollouts of the same task under matched interventions on the talent-conditioned context, and optimizes downstream execution with group-relative advantages. Across seven open-domain and multi-hop question-answering benchmarks, talent-aware supervised fine-tuning improves average success rate by 7.53%, 6.71%, and 9.21% over the corresponding zero-shot baselines for Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3-8B, and Llama-3.1-8B, respectively. Reinforcement learning further improves these SFT checkpoints by 7.58% to 9.32%, and TalentRL achieves the highest average success rate among all compared methods on all three backbones. On the agentic benchmarks ALFWorld and WebShop, TalentRL achieves the highest ALFWorld success rate (99.3%) and the highest WebShop score (90.7) among all compared methods.
  </details>

- **[bf16 Rounding Silently Discards Most RL Policy Updates: A Hidden Training-Inference Mismatch and an Unbiased One-Line Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41073)**  `arXiv:2610.41073`  
  _Andrew Gao, William Gao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training of language models keeps fp32 master weights in the optimizer and serves rollouts from a bf16 copy produced by round-to-nearest conversion. At the learning rates in common use, most per-step updates are smaller than half a bf16 spacing and the conversion discards them: on Qwen3-1.7B only 0.4-0.5% of served weights change per step, and the policy that generates the training data lags the optimizer by 50-150 steps. Existing mismatch corrections cannot see this lag because they compare two copies downstream of the same rounding. We propose Fair Cast: export the master with an unbiased rounding rule (stochastic rounding or error feedback) and compute the training loss on the served copy. The change is one line at the export call, adds no hyperparameters and costs 22-38 ms per export of 1.7B parameters. Across 25 matched seed-pairs on Qwen3-0.6B to 4B with AdamW and Muon, Fair Cast is ahead on 18, tied on 6 and behind on 1, with a mean gain of 6.1 points; on zebra puzzles it reaches 0.57 +/- 0.16 over four runs where round-to-nearest collapses to 0.33 +/- 0.10. It also makes the saved fp32 checkpoint agree with the served model, and it exposes the implicit lag of round-to-nearest as a regularizer that can be restored explicitly when a task needs it.
  </details>

- **[Before We Adapt: The Zero-Shot Generalization Bottleneck in Continual World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41041)**  `arXiv:2610.41041`  
  _Amir Mesbah, Di Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) requires agents to operate under environmental changes while reusing prior knowledge whenever possible. We argue that model-based reinforcement learning provides a promising framework for this setting because learned world models (WMs) can, in principle, support change detection, knowledge reuse, adaptation, and acquisition of new knowledge. However, such benefits depend heavily on whether the learned model can generalize beyond the contexts in which it was trained. In this work, we propose to formulate this problem with a contextual Markov decision process in which context is decomposed into behavioral and physical components. This view organizes continual WM learning into reuse, adaptation, and acquisition of new knowledge. As a first step, we study direct reuse when behavioral rules remain unchanged. Using Dyna-Q and TD-MPC in controlled FourRooms tasks, we find that zero-shot reuse can degrade substantially under physical-context changes and that this behavior depends strongly on the observation representation used by the WM. Additional ablation studies and latent-space diagnostics suggest that learned representations do not automatically provide the invariances needed for reuse. These results identify representation generalization as an important bottleneck for reliable world-model reuse in CRL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Understanding Score-Seeking in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41014)**  `arXiv:2610.41014`  
  _Aghyad Deeb, Ryan Greenblatt, Alex Mallen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Production large language models sometimes reward-hack, exploiting flaws in how tasks are scored rather than performing the tasks as intended, sometimes in highly sophisticated and adversarial ways. We investigate the motivations driving this behavior in GPT 5.6 Sol, GPT 5.5, and a checkpoint of OpenAI o3 without safety training ("exp-rl-cap-late") by observing model behavior in agentic environments where the grader is leaked. In our environments, the models appear to be score-seeking: trying to score well according to graders that they believe assess their current actions, even at the expense of violating explicit user instructions. They appear to care about score itself rather than its downstream consequences, violating instructions at approximately the same rate whether the score is indicated to be used for training, for evaluation, or not at all. We observe evidence that the more recent models care to some extent about scores that do not assess their own current actions. GPT 5.6 Sol and GPT 5.5 help other agents 24.6 percentage points more often on average when the other agent stands to gain a score of 1 rather than 0 from being helped. However, when grading or monitoring another trajectory, we find models never collude by distorting its score.
  </details>

- **[When Can Unlabeled Data Pools Replace Membership Queries?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41001)**  `arXiv:2610.41001`  
  _Dravyansh Sharma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Membership queries let a learner label arbitrary points and give near-optimal $\tilde O(n\log(1/\eps))$ label complexity for halfspaces under every marginal. A pool learner may label only sampled points. We ask when unlabeled data can replace membership queries. For homogeneous halfspaces the answer depends only on the angular law of $X/\|X\|$. If this law assigns zero mass to every hyperplane through the origin and positive mass to every nonempty open set of directions together with its reflection, a pool learner uses $O(n\log^3n\log(n/(\eps\delta)))$ labels, uniformly over all such marginals and targets, without density, moment, concavity or isotropy assumptions. The same label bound holds for non-homogeneous halfspaces under every marginal with full support and no mass on affine hyperplanes, including Gaussians. Dropping either angular assumption admits $\Omega(1/\eps)$ pool versus $O(\log(1/\eps))$ membership-query separations, even with unlimited unlabeled data. The price is paid in unlabeled data: in dimension $n\ge3$, for every unlabeled budget fixed in advance, some marginal satisfying both assumptions forces $\Omega(1/\eps)$ labels. For isotropic $s$-concave marginals in dimension $n\ge3$, unlimited pools keep logarithmic label rates for every finite $s$. With an unlabeled budget fixed in advance, class-uniform polylogarithmic rates hold exactly for $s>-1/(n+1)$, or $s>-1/n$ under central symmetry, and in the general class the optimal rate between these thresholds is $\tilde\Theta(\eps^{-(n-2)/n})$. This extends the range $s\ge-1/(2n+3)$ of previous sample-based guarantees, in the realizable setting.
  </details>

- **[Steering Vectors as a Training Signal in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40998)**  `arXiv:2610.40998`  
  _Tiejin Chen, Maunil Vyas, Huaiyuan Yao, Hua Wei_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Steering vectors (SVs) are residual-stream directions that shift the behavior of LLMs when added to hidden states at inference time. While prior work has mainly used SVs as post-hoc controls, we ask whether the same directions can also guide parameter updates during post-training. We study this question across supervised learning, including SFT and distillation, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Our framework considers two intervention regimes: a hidden-state hook during teacher-forced supervised training, and an advantage-repair mechanism on wrong rollouts in GRPO. For each regime, we analyze three design axes, which are the steering direction, its placement, and the operation used to combine it with training. In supervised settings, SV injection improves over plain SFT and distillation, with gains scaling with the off-policy gap and reaching up to a $12\%$ improvement when distilling from thinking-mode teachers. These gains hold across three random seeds. In RL settings, applying a correctness-contrast direction as advantage repair on wrong rollouts, including otherwise zero-gradient all-wrong groups, recovers a learning signal that transfers to held-out math benchmarks and acts as a composable auxiliary signal that adds to existing zero-variance remedies such as entropy-aware advantage shaping. We further identify three failure boundaries. In detail, code generation can break the direction, cross-family layer transfer can break the placement, and inference-time application on an SV-trained checkpoint can break the intervention regime. Together, these results establish steering vectors as a conditional but effective training signal for LLM post-training. Our code is available here.
  </details>

- **[Monte Carlo Random Walk Kernel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40956)**  `arXiv:2610.40956`  
  _Arslan Iamaletdinov, Elfat Sabitov, Konstantin Sorokin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce the Monte Carlo Random Walk Kernel (MCRWK), an estimator whose per-pair cost is independent of graph size and derived directly from the random walk kernel (RWK) expansion. By normalizing the kernel coefficients, MCRWK samples a shared walk length and estimates its contribution using random walks on the input graphs. This gives a simple way to approximate RWK without constructing the product graph. For a fixed expected walk length and efficient neighbor sampling, each pairwise estimate takes $O(m)$ time for $m$ Monte Carlo samples, allowing the sample budget to control the trade-off between runtime and accuracy. We evaluate MCRWK against Graph Voyagers and classical RWK methods on synthetic and real graph datasets. In the reported experiments on large synthetic graphs, MCRWK is faster than Graph Voyagers and, at matched runtime, more accurate. It also has the lowest Gram-matrix runtime on the five real datasets studied. Code and experiments are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[CAMEL: Cross-Architecture Masked Embedding Learning for Ultra-Lightweight Retinal Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40954)**  `arXiv:2610.40954`  
  _Andre Guarnier De Mitri, Giovani Decico Lucafo, Miller Matheus Lima Anacleto Rocha, Diego Furtado Silva_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent progress in deep learning has substantially advanced automated grading of ophthalmologic pathologies, supported by large annotated datasets and improved representation learning. However, a significant gap remains between state-of-the-art performance and clinical scalability. In particular, foundation models have reshaped image-based medical screening and diagnosis. These large-scale architectures trained on extensive datasets learn rich, generalizable feature representations that can be adapted to a wide range of downstream tasks. In ophthalmology, RETFound, one of the most relevant foundation models for retinal images, performs strongly across classification, segmentation, and risk prediction tasks. Despite their effectiveness, foundation models are often computationally prohibitive, hindering their deployment in real-world clinical environments where hardware constraints and latency are critical. To bridge this gap, we introduce CAMEL (Cross-Architecture Masked Embedding Learning), a novel cross-architecture distillation framework that uses an asymmetric masking strategy during embedding learning to effectively transfer knowledge from massive teacher foundation models to compact, lightweight student models. Our experimental evaluation across four datasets shows that CAMEL preserves accuracy while drastically reducing computational complexity, enabling lightweight yet highly accurate models suitable for resource-limited clinical settings.
  </details>

- **[On the Origin and Evolution of Training-Inference Mismatch in LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40945)**  `arXiv:2610.40945`  
  _Yu Li, Yufa Zhou, Tian Lan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy reinforcement learning for large language models assumes that training responses are sampled from the current policy. In practice, an inference engine samples the responses and a separate trainer computes the gradients, and the \textbf{training-inference mismatch} between the two makes the training data off-policy. The mismatch has two sources, the numerics of the engine and the stale weights of asynchronous sampling, and a correction that stabilizes training under one source can fail under the other. \textit{We ask which property of a mismatch source decides the correction it needs.} Evaluating the trainer at the sampler's weights splits each sampled-token error exactly into a \textit{numerical discrepancy} of the engine and a \textit{policy lag} of the trainer, and we follow both through training. The numerical discrepancy stays approximately constant, so it biases the updates by a nearly fixed amount, and centering the score at each prefix removes its leading bias. The policy lag is produced by the trainer's own updates: the bias it leaves enters the next update, which enlarges the next lag and closes a feedback loop. Importance weighting cuts this loop only while the lag ratio stays concentrated, and on Qwen3-1.7B it fails at the step where the effective sample size of the ratio falls below one half. From this account we derive the \textbf{discrepancy-lag correction} (DLC), which centers the numerical discrepancy, clips the lag ratio against the sampler's weights and resynchronizes the sampler when this effective sample size falls below one half. Under a stale sampler, DLC keeps the KL divergence from on-policy training within twice the KL between two independent on-policy trainings on Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-1.7B, and its resynchronization prevents collapses that the per-token corrections leave and that a KL measured on the sampler's prefixes does not detect.
  </details>

- **[A Theoretical Framework for Understanding and Building "Any-task inference" Capable Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40934)**  `arXiv:2610.40934`  
  _Matthew McDermott, Florent Pollet, Payal Chandak, Gregory Kondas, Liat Friedman Antwarg, Noa Dagan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we provide a formal framework and mathematical definition for the subset of foundation models focused on enabling the property of "any-task inference" (ATI) (similar to zero-shot inference), rather than those designed for pre-training fine-tuning. To achieve this mathematical definition, we formalize the notion of a "space of tasks of interest," which we include in the problem domain, then provide an interface-based formal definition for ATI foundation models as those that map samples of data $x$ and tasks $t$ to predictions over the space of labels directly. While simple, this definition allows us to show that all ATI foundation models can be decomposed through a "universal task reduction" lens into encoder, model, and decoder aspects. We can leverage this decomposition to also decompose foundation model error into terms corresponding to different aspects of this reduction, stratified into a collection of "design terms," which are theoretical properties that can inform one of whether a particular proposed ATI foundation model can achieve optimality or not due to the structure of the proposed task reduction, and "realized model excess" terms which capture interactions between learned and programmatic components of foundation model systems. Lastly, we can use this perspective to offer two "prototypical" recipes for realizing ATI foundation models in diverse domains, which we term generative and discriminative variants of ATI foundation models. This exercise is grounded throughout by 5 real-world examples of foundation models in different settings, varying greatly in how these capabilities are realized, showing the applicability of this definition to real-world settings.
  </details>

- **[No Channels Left Behind: Understanding Diffusion Acceleration through Residual Gates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40914)**  `arXiv:2610.40914`  
  _Junha Park, Jaemyung Yu, Jaehui Hwang, Jong-Seok Lee, Dongyoon Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training acceleration methods for diffusion transformers, such as REPA and dispersive regularization, are commonly explained by additional supervision, spatial-structure priors, or improved feature geometry. We revisit this view and uncover a striking internal change: the AdaLN-Zero residual gate $\alpha$, which directly controls how conditioned updates enter the residual stream, is substantially reorganized by these methods. This observation motivates our hypothesis that acceleration arises, at least in part, from reshaping the residual gating pathway rather than solely from the feature structures explicitly imposed by auxiliary objectives. Supporting this view, accelerated models are markedly more sensitive to perturbations of $\alpha$, and their gate geometry reflects semantic relationships among classes. We further introduce the normalized participation ratio (nPR) to characterize the channel-wise organization of the gates and find that, across REPA teachers, projector architectures, and radial-objective targets, broader gate participation accompanies better generation quality. Guided by this insight, we propose a simple teacher-free radial objective that promotes broader gate participation through feature-norm regularization, without pairwise feature comparisons. On ImageNet $256\times256$ with SiT-B/2, our method improves FID over dispersive loss while increasing mean gate participation over timesteps and classes, further supporting the link between gate organization and training acceleration. Our results identify residual-gate reorganization as a measurable axis of diffusion training acceleration and suggest a simple direction for further acceleration.
  </details>

- **[TSFLab: A Self-Improving Agent-Agnostic Workbench for Time Series Forecasting Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40901)**  `arXiv:2610.40901`  
  _ChengAo Shen, Yanjun Zhao, Haoxin Liu, Bonnie Chen, Miles Ma, Dongjin Song, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As publications and products on time series forecasting (TSF) proliferate, researchers face a growing burden of analyzing existing work. First, not all publications release code. For instance, only about 20% of arXiv TSF papers have a code link in the surveyed period. Second, released code also requires manual maintenance. Thus, TSF researchers must constantly adapt or implement existing works. However, independent implementations can differ in experimental settings and are difficult to maintain. We therefore propose to use AI agents for benchmarking TSF research and even supporting autoresearch, e.g., developing new TSF models. Yet directly applying existing coding agents, such as Claude Code and Codex, is suboptimal: prohibitive costs, limited scalability to large research projects (e.g., benchmarking), and lack of self-maintenance. To address these challenges, we propose TSFLab, a self-improving agent-agnostic "workbench" that equips compatible coding agents with shared resources and tools for low-cost TSF research and continuous maintenance of research resources. Our experiments cover reproduction, benchmarking, and new method development. In reproduction and benchmarking, TSFLab improves task completion by 20-163% and cuts fresh tokens per completed task by 11-66% across baselines. A pilot study of agent-driven method development also shows improved forecasting accuracy via TSFLab, with 32.8-73.6% fewer fresh tokens and 9.2-68.8% less development time than the other settings.
  </details>

- **[TensorSSM: Memorizing Spatial Position in Dynamic State Space for Tensor Core Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40871)**  `arXiv:2610.40871`  
  _Sheng Zhang, Xinyi Wang, Maolin Wang, Xiangyu Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-dimensional tensors are ubiquitous in real-world applications but lead to massive storage overhead. Functional tensor decomposition addresses this by employing neural networks to map element coordinates to tensor cores, significantly reducing the parameters for data compression. However, existing functional methods typically flatten high-order tensors into 1D sequences. This folding operation causes highly overlapping local coordinate sequences and struggles to capture complex cross-mode spatial dependencies, severely limiting their expressiveness on diverse datasets. To tackle these challenges, we propose TensorSSM, a novel Implicit Neural Representation (INR) for functional decomposition method that memorizes spatial positions within a dynamic State Space Model (SSM). Specifically, TensorSSM first decomposes mode-wise coordinates into sequences spit by K-ary rule, encoding spatial tokens from coarse to fine, which effectively captures spatial variations. Furthermore, we extend the SSM's state space to explicitly map cross-mode spatial dependencies, allowing inter-mode interactions to enhance the updates of each mode. By integrating cross-mode information from previous hidden states into the current state transitions, TensorSSM effectively expands tensor representation along two dimensions: the sequence dimension for coarse-to-fine spatial tokens, and the state dimension for cross-mode memorization. This design effectively integrates spatial information into the state space and the tensor core, substantially advancing the state adaptability and enhancing the expressiveness of the data compression. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TensorSSM achieves significantly high reconstruction accuracy and storage efficiency, exhibiting robust expressiveness across diverse tensor datasets without relying on strict spatial assumptions.
  </details>

- **[Unsupervised Feature Selection Using Local Intrinsic Dimensionality -- Extended Version](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40736)**  `arXiv:2610.40736`  
  _Oussama Chelly, Michael E. Houle, Ken-ichi Kawarabayashi, Muhammad Rajabinasab, Arthur Zimek_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High dimensionality degrades both the efficiency and the effectiveness of learning algorithms, and in many applications no class labels are available to guide dimensionality reduction. This paper proposes LIDFS, an unsupervised feature selection method based on Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID). The method builds on the formal equivalence between LID and the indiscriminability of distance measures. The data matrix is transposed so that each feature is treated as a point, and the LID of each feature is estimated by maximum likelihood from the distances to its $k$ nearest neighboring features. Features with lower LID exhibit greater local discriminability and receive higher importance scores. A refinement step then retains one representative per feature neighborhood and demotes locally redundant features. The result is a complete ranking from which any number of features can be selected without rerunning the procedure. LIDFS does not use any information about labels or the number of classes, and runs in $\mathcal{O}(d^2 n)$ time for $n$ instances and $d$ features. LIDFS is compared with classic and state-of-the-art methods and a random baseline on 23 high-dimensional datasets, the classic benchmark collection. The evaluation uses classification accuracy and AUC with five downstream classifiers, as well as clustering accuracy and normalized mutual information, over both full and aggressive selection ranges. LIDFS ranks first in the supervised evaluations and first or tied second in the clustering-based evaluations. It belongs to the top statistical group together with methods that require the number of classes, and it runs faster than its closest competitor. Several recent methods, by contrast, fail to outperform the random baseline. A sensitivity analysis shows that small neighborhood sizes yield the best results.
  </details>

- **[Don't Just Impute, Detect: Task-Aware Diffusion Pipelines for Edge Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40732)**  `arXiv:2610.40732`  
  _Jeongsu Park, Taehyeon Yun, Geonwoo Kim, Yuji Lim, Moohong Min_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial Internet of Things (IIoT) edge pipelines routinely lose contiguous blocks of multi-sensor data to network dropouts and hardware faults. The standard remedy is a disjointed "impute-then-detect" pipeline whose imputer is trained only for Mean Squared Error (MSE). Such an imputer fills a missing block with the most probable normal pattern: a partially observed anomaly is smoothed back toward normality, and boundary artifacts on normal data raise false alarms. We call this imputation bias and propose the Task-Aware Diffusion Refiner (TADR), a conditional diffusion refiner trained against the loss landscape of a frozen downstream detector. Because back-propagating through the sampling trajectory is intractable, the task-aware terms act on the one-step clean-window estimate that the noise prediction already yields, so training costs no more than plain diffusion training. Under strict Point-to-Point evaluation over five seeds with paired significance tests, TADR raises F1 by $0.338$ over the unrefined detector on SMD ($p = 0.0015$), and the anomaly enhancement objective alone contributes $0.463$ in ablation. We also characterise when the method helps: the gain tracks the continuous, mutually redundant channels available and the anomaly evidence the outage removes, and it is absent on telemetry datasets dominated by one-hot command channels and on PSM. At ten sampling steps TADR runs in $41.4$ ms per window on a single CPU core, sustaining about $24$ concurrent 1 Hz streams from a $4.03$ MB model. Code and per-seed results are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[NECA: Neighbor Event Curve Augmentation for Multi-Horizon ICU Deterioration Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40420)**  `arXiv:2610.40420`  
  _Chenyang Wang, Theresa Jiawei Tang, Xinran Liu, Xinyu Lu, Xulei Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early prediction of ICU organ dysfunction events supports timely clinical monitoring and intervention, but estimating short-term organ-specific risk from early ICU records remains challenging due to irregular measurements, heterogeneous clinical variables, and the need to predict risk across multiple future horizons. Existing supervised models usually treat each patient as an isolated sample and do not explicitly use the observed event timing of similar historical patients. We propose Neighbor Event-Curve Augmentation (NECA), a model-agnostic method that retrieves training-set patients with similar early-window profiles and converts their subsequent organ dysfunction event onset times into cumulative event-curve features over 6h, 12h, 24h, and 42h. These features are concatenated with the original patient representation and used by standard downstream predictors. On PhysioNet 2012 and a 4,000-stay MIMIC-IV subset, NECA improves average PR-AUC and ROC-AUC across four organ dysfunction event tasks and seven supervised learners. The largest gain appears for neurological dysfunction events, where task-level average PR-AUC increases from 0.461 to 0.557 on PhysioNet 2012. Ablations show that clinically meaningful retrieval and horizon-specific neighbor risk drive most of the improvement, while the full cumulative curve adds task-dependent temporal context. These results support similar-patient event timing as a structured temporal risk signal for ICU organ dysfunction event prediction. The source code will be publicly released upon acceptance of this paper.
  </details>

- **[Turing LLM Post-Training: Calibrated Distillation and Efficient Dynamic-MoE Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39876)**  `arXiv:2610.39876`  
  _Yue He, Jiahui Hu, Shaman Tang, Hua Zhou, Qiman Wu, Menglin Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study post-training for dynamically routed mixture-of-experts (MoE) models, focusing on integrating independently optimized specialists while keeping consolidation efficient. Our approach combines calibrated multi-teacher on-policy distillation (C-MOPD), which matches each selected teacher on a local Top-$K$ support with teacher-wise loss normalization and clipping, with an execution path that uses post-capacity bounded-width dispatch, final-route replay, and asynchronous rollout--training overlap. On Turing-20B-A2B, the selected model achieves a four-domain normalized integration score of 0.9988 and retains at least 90.16\% of the measured specialist gain in every domain. At matched $K=32$, the full calibration objective scores 0.9962, compared with 0.9724 without calibration and 0.9834 without clipping. At a matched 8K response cap, the model exceeds both shared SFT and Qwen3-8B on all four reasoning-enabled mathematics and code benchmarks (unweighted mean: 85.92, versus 80.33 for Qwen3-8B and 74.21 for SFT), and improves over SFT on ten of twelve broader evaluation metrics. In a PPU-based profile, the combined execution configuration reduces consolidation step time from 398.7 to 139.3 seconds, an observed 2.86$\times$ speedup over serial execution. These results demonstrate strong specialist integration alongside more efficient consolidation, while reflecting the complete training and execution framework rather than any single component.
  </details>

- **[TaxoMFM: A Taxonomy-Aware Foundation Model for the Human Gut Microbiome](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39316)**  `arXiv:2610.39316`  
  _Haochen Xue, Anastasia Godneva, Yulong Li, Qi Cao, Zhaodong Wu, Kaiyu Liang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing gut microbiome foundation models typically use flat taxon representations, limiting their ability to exploit known taxonomic hierarchies. We introduce TaxoMFM, a microbiome foundation model that integrates taxonomic structure into self-supervised learning. TaxoMFM jointly learns abundance patterns across taxonomic ranks and relative proportions within groups through taxonomy-aware encoding and hierarchical compositional reconstruction. We further construct TaxoGut-HPP, comprising 100,806 gut microbiome profiles from 231 source cohorts and covering 40 classification and 8 regression tasks. TaxoMFM achieves competitive performance across disease, gut health, host trait, and liver ultrasound phenotype prediction. In cross-cohort zero-shot transfer, where target cohorts are excluded from both pretraining and downstream adaptation, TaxoMFM achieves AUROCs of 0.838, 0.736, and 0.722 for colorectal cancer, type 2 diabetes, and inflammatory bowel disease, respectively. Further analyses show that TaxoMFM recovers community changes across taxonomic scales, while preserving community information and producing consistent representations under database updates and species-vocabulary shifts. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Normative Deviation Modeling for EEG-based Depression Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37338)**  `arXiv:2610.37338`  
  _Wan Jiang, Jingqi Cheng, Hui Zhang, Xu Zhao, Xiaojing Chen, Shengeng Tang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web-enabled digital health services are expanding access to physiological monitoring and mental health screening. However, electroencephalography (EEG)-based major depressive disorder (MDD) detection remains challenging across heterogeneous data sources, where individual variability and acquisition differences may affect disease-related representations. In this work, we investigate the use of healthy normative references for EEG-based MDD detection. Specifically, structural and distributional reference models are independently trained using healthy control (HC) recordings to characterize departures from healthy EEG patterns. We develop two diagnostic approaches that incorporate normative deviations at different representation levels. Scalar-level Normative Deviation-guided Diagnosis (S-NDD) summarizes structural and distributional deviations into scalar scores and integrates them with deep EEG representations. Feature-level Normative Deviation-guided Diagnosis (F-NDD) retains dimension-wise residuals and combines them with deep EEG representations through attention-based aggregation and auxiliary supervision. We also employ target HC-assisted score calibration for cross-source evaluation without updating the trained models. Experiments on two public EEG datasets examine both approaches under subject-independent within-source and bidirectional cross-source settings. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of normative deviation-guided diagnosis across different evaluation settings.
  </details>

- **[Cumulative Marginal Matching for One-Step Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34575)**  `arXiv:2610.34575`  
  _Paribesh Regmi, Rui Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The sampling procedures of diffusion and flow-matching models involve hundreds of denoising steps incurring substantial inference time, which has motivated a growing body of work on training schemes for few-step and one-step generation. Existing approaches primarily seek to align the generation trajectory of few-step and base models, yet their training objectives only involve discrepancies at a single point in the trajectory, encouraging only local \emph{point-level} alignment. While recent methods such as \emph{Shortcut-CSL} introduce cumulative objective that enforce alignment over the entire trajectory, this is suboptimal as they do not directly enforce alignment between the distributions induced by the two models throughout the generation process. We propose \emph{Cumulative Marginal Matching} (CMM), a principled objective for few-step generation: by viewing flow-matching as a sequence of intermediate marginal distributions connecting the prior and data distributions, CMM directly optimizes the cumulative discrepancy between the base and few-step marginal distributions over the entire sequence. Specifically, we formulate this discrepancy using the Wasserstein-2 distance. By exploiting the pushforward representation of flow-matching, we transform an intractable cumulative Wasserstein objective into a tractable surrogate that can be optimized using samples from the intermediate marginal, which are readily generated through the conditional interpolation process of flow-matching. Furthermore, we show that the existing \emph{Shortcut-CSL} objective arises as a first-order approximation of that of the CMM, establishing a theoretical connection between the two objectives. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate improvement in generation quality over \emph{Shortcut-CSL} and other state-of-the-art one- and few-step baselines under identical training budgets.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Cooperative GFlowNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33968)**  `arXiv:2610.33968`  
  _Pedro Dall'Antonia, Juan Belieni, Ana Carolina Erthal, Saullo H. G. de Oliveira, Eliezer de Souza da Silva, Diego Mesquita_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) are trained to sample objects from compositional spaces with probability proportional to a reward function, but ineffective exploration can cause learned samplers to concentrate on a few high-reward regions. An intuitive remedy is to train multiple GFlowNets independently in parallel and combine their samples. However, this does not prevent multiple models from collapsing onto the same high-reward areas, leaving large regions of the state space unexplored. With this in mind, we propose Hierarchical Cooperative GFlowNets (HCG), an approach that dynamically partitions the search space to coordinate discovery without relying on hand-crafted boundaries. HCG imposes an ordering in which the highest-ranked model may explore the entire space, while lower-ranked models are progressively pushed toward regions not covered by higher-ranked ones. We provide a theoretical characterization of how specific learning objectives can shift probability mass away from designated areas, instantiating this mechanism within our architecture. A shared classifier coordinates the hierarchy by partitioning the search space, keeping the GFlowNets independently parameterized and enabling asynchronous training. Furthermore, we derive a normalizer-weighted aggregation rule for the ensemble and prove that it recovers the target reward-proportional distribution under the stated assumptions. Finally, experiments across multimodal distributions, sequence generation, combinatorial optimization, and biological sequence design show that HCG improves mode coverage and target approximation, translating increases in the number of coordinated samplers into broader, complementary exploration.
  </details>

- **[When Does Dynamics Randomization Test Context Inference?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32414)**  `arXiv:2610.32414`  
  _Oleg Smirnov, Sofiane Ennadir, John Pertoft, Bjartur Hjaltason, Sara Karimi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Environments with randomized dynamics are widely used to ask whether world models need explicit context inference. However, randomization can leave little predictive information for a context encoder to add, because distinct latent parameters may produce similar transitions at visited states, or those states may already reveal the active context. We formalize the remaining one-step information as predictive headroom and compare kernels under fixed and visited state--action distributions to distinguish changes in query coverage from changes in context weights. We analyze discrete and continuous environments and find low headroom both when kernels differ little and when visitation reduces an otherwise large discrepancy. At the same time, resetting state components increases headroom, showing that context evidence accumulates in state. We propose reporting this headroom alongside the randomized parameters, so that gains from explicit context inference can be interpreted relative to the information missing from the current state and action.
  </details>

- **[Estimating Performance for Safe Deployment of Machine Learning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29262)**  `arXiv:2610.29262`  
  _Zeju Li, Konstantinos Kamnitsas, Mobarak I. Hoque, Melanie Roschewitz, Cheng Ouyang, Chen Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The main challenge in applying deep neural networks to real-world scenarios lies in distributional discrepancies between training and test datasets, often caused by variations in data acquisition conditions. Assessing the performance of pre-trained models without labels is crucial for safety, but this is complicated by deep models' overly optimistic probabilistic outputs, often exacerbated by class imbalances, leading to unreliable predictions. To address these issues, we introduce MOVAL, a novel MOdel eVALuation tool that estimates performance using unlabeled test data. MOVAL incorporates class-wise confidence calibration to adjust model outputs, allowing for the estimation of metrics beyond accuracy and mitigating the detrimental effects of class imbalance on confidence calibration. Additionally, we propose an ensemble approach that combines multiple calibrated confidence scores for a more robust solution. We validate MOVAL on natural and medical image datasets with various acquisition shifts, covering tasks from classification to segmentation. It achieves state-of-the-art results across evaluation metrics such as accuracy, F1-Score, AUC, and more. Furthermore, we demonstrate that MOVAL provides accurate performance estimations at the class, instance, and pixel levels, ensuring the transparent and safe deployment of machine learning models. We provide MOVAL as a deployment tool for monitoring and estimating performance under a range of domain shifts.
  </details>

- **[Transport What Shares the Conduction Pathway: Normal-Anchored SPD Geometric Transport for Source-Free Patient Adaptation of Holter and Patch ECG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24430)**  `arXiv:2610.24430`  
  _JinKook Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous electrocardiogram (ECG) streams are dominated by normal beats (87.9-98.6% of the label-free adaptation prefix in our four databases), and patient-specific waveform distortion is not shared by every beat class; under this combination, source-free and test-time adaptation methods that assume class-balanced target batches collapse. We propose Normal-Anchored Geometric Transport (NAGT, GA_NS), which estimates a closed-form symmetric positive-definite congruence map from label-free normal anchors in the first 5 min of each recording and applies it only to the normal and supraventricular prototypes, keeping the ventricular and fusion prototypes fixed. Decision rules were set on MIT-BIH and the rate-invariant RR head was adopted after the development databases (INCART, SVDB) had been opened; the frozen model was then evaluated once under a pre-specified locked evaluation protocol on the held-out Icentia11k chest-patch cohort. In 493 patients (973 recordings, 3,615,388 evaluated beats), NAGT raised the arrhythmia macro-F1 J from 34.5% to 48.7% ($\Delta J$ = +14.1 [+8.6, +19.3] pp; non-AF 43.9% to 59.8%) and outperformed all five Holm-adjusted comparators, including AdaBN, Tent and T3A. In post hoc analyses of the frozen outputs, ventricular false alarms fell by 68% (41.1 to 13.2 per hour) and 98.3% of normal beats stayed outside the alarm classes. Adaptation takes 5.8 ms per record without back-propagation.
  </details>

- **[TILE: Bridging LLMs and Table Foundation Models for Tabular Learning with Strings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17065)**  `arXiv:2610.17065`  
  _Félix Lefebvre, David Holzmüller, Gioia Blayer, Myung Jun Kim, Marine Le Morvan, Gaël Varoquaux_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world tables are often heterogeneous, combining numerical features with free-form string columns. Table foundation models (TFMs) excel on numerical tables but require strings to be encoded numerically, while large language models (LLMs) hold semantic knowledge but are not designed for tabular predictions. Most existing bridges use LLMs as plug-in encoders for cell strings, yet how to extract representations effectively for tabular learning remains underexplored. We show that the interface between LLMs and tables matters as much as the LLM itself. Applied cell-wise, larger LLMs do not give better features. With our interface, the same LLMs improve steadily with size. TILE (Table-Interfaced LLM Embeddings) combines four ingredients: row serialization to provide cross-column context, intermediate-layer extraction to avoid layers specialized for next-token prediction, columnar pooling to remove mean pooling's length bias, and by-pass features to route numbers and categories past the LLM. Paired with TFMs, TILE sets a new state of the art for tables with strings. Across 100 datasets, it beats the strongest baseline on $90\%$ of splits, with gains across 7 LLMs and 10 tabular learners, and ranks first on STRABLE's 108 datasets.
  </details>

- **[Are We Really Benchmarking Forecasting Models? The Impact of Preprocessing on Time Series Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09096)**  `arXiv:2610.09096`  
  _Guilherme Afonso Galindo Padilha, Paulo S. G. de Mattos Neto, Rafael M. O. Cruz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While established literature underscores the pivotal role of preprocessing in forecasting accuracy, this stage remains largely overlooked in current research. Modern benchmarks typically resort to simple scaling, failing to account for critical transformations required to address nonstationarity, such as differencing. This omission creates a significant structural preprocessing bias that favors models with built-in data treatments while obscuring the true potential of simpler architectures. We study this effect through a preprocessing-aware benchmark that evaluates 11 forecasting models across 16 reversible preprocessing pipelines on 29,000 M4 time series. Our results identify preprocessing as a key driver of forecasting performance. Optimizing preprocessing per series yields gains of approximately 27\% to 87\% across all evaluated models, with architectures lacking internalized preprocessing experiencing the most substantial improvements. This allows simpler architectures to become highly competitive with complex, state-of-the-art models in modern forecasting benchmarks. All resources and experimental results from this benchmark are stored in a comprehensive metadataset to support future metalearning tasks.
  </details>

- **[SkillEvoLean: Mutation-enhanced skill evolution for Lean provers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01799)**  `arXiv:2610.01799`  
  _Kuo Zhou, Zixiong Yang, Lu Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill evolution offers a promising way to improve large language model agents without updating their parameters, but its use in formal theorem proving remains underexplored. Existing methods mainly target natural-language reasoning, improving skills by analyzing successful and failed trajectories and incrementally revising solving strategies. Although the Lean verifier provides reliable execution feedback, when all sampled trajectories fail, existing skill evolution methods lack successful trajectories from which to infer effective update directions. Furthermore, these methods also focus mainly on the root instruction file, thus underexploring the evolution of reference knowledge including mathematical concepts and proving techniques. To address these limitations, we propose a mutation-enhanced skill self-evolution framework for building skill-augmented Lean provers. The framework jointly evolves a high-level solving policy and its reference knowledge through progressive and mutation-based updates. Progressive evolution derives local improvements from successful and failed trajectories, while mutation is triggered when no complete proof can be generated, sampling mathematical concepts to produce and select new skill candidates under verifier feedback. We evaluate our method on MiniF2F, PutnamBench, the 2025 International Mathematical Olympiad (IMO 2025), and the 2026 USA Mathematical Olympiad (USAMO 2026). Under the same backbone model, trajectorysampling budget, and test-time compute, our method achieves proof success rates of 100.0%, 90.6%, 4/6, and 4/6, respectively, with GPT-5.5, outperforming the baseline methods. Further analysis shows that concept-guided mutation outperforms random-text-guided mutation by 6.9 and 8.2 percentage points on MiniF2F and PutnamBench, respectively, while solving one additional problem on both IMO 2025 and USAMO 2026.
  </details>

- **[Non-Commutative Lie-Algebraic State-Space Models: Finite-Division Variety Laws, Semidirect Cascades, and Gradient Saddle Escape](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00329)**  `arXiv:2610.00329`  
  _Zeyu Jia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured state-space sequence models (SSMs) achieve linear-time inference and logarithmic-time parallel training via associative scans, yet diagonal transitions generate solvable semigroups unable to track non-solvable group words or 3D rigid-body manifolds. We present Non-Commutative Lie-Algebraic State-Space Models (Lie-SSMs), parameterizing recurrent updates on Lie algebra tangent spaces via the matrix exponential. First, using an o-minimal cylindrical flag collar, we establish an unconditional single-layer finite-division criterion for contracting $SO(3)$ and $SU(2)$ heads, proving odd-prime Sylow abelianity, odd-$p$-core centrality, and exponent-30 commutator obstructions excluding $S_5$ in $SO(3)^m, SU(2)^m,$ and $SO(4)^m$ while identifying the sharp threshold $d^*(S_5)=d^*(A_6)=5$ in $SO(5)$. Second, Depth-2 semidirect and Schreier $2$-cocycle cascades resolve $S_5$, $2I\cong SL_2(F_5)$, and $\mathrm{Heis}(F_p)$ in $O(\log T)$ scan depth, whereas unnormalized contracting coupling collapses exponentially without Directional RMSNorm. Third, we characterize the $\Theta(\varepsilon^{c-1})$ Chen--Magnus--Hall--Witt commutator saddle and $1/|[G,G]|$ coset-centroid dilution, proving generic first-order symmetry breaking at $\varepsilon=0$ and teacher-forced local Polyak--Łojasiewicz convergence under dense prefix supervision and factored readouts ($99.90\%/99.40\%$ on $S_5$ at $T=128$; $100.00\%$ on $D_{16}$). Across $SE_2(3)$ Left-Jacobian odometry, Hillis--Steele/Blelloch GPU scans ($48.24\times$ forward, $20.27\times/10.28\times$ full-layer speedup at $T=2048$), and three 3D IMU benchmarks (UCI HAR, UCI HAPT-PT, and 17-subject REALDISP), Lie-$SO(3)$ models reach $95.69\%\pm 0.14\%$ on HAR, $88.44\%\pm 0.83\%$ episode logit-vote ($83.97\%\pm 1.20\%$ C3 window in 30-subject 5-fold CV; $87.16\%\pm 0.64\%$ 9-subject split) on HAPT-PT, and $84.23\%\pm 1.00\%$ on REALDISP.
  </details>

- **[A Comprehensive View of Fairness through Distributional Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37061)**  `arXiv:2609.37061`  
  _Gayane Taturyan, Charlotte Laclau, Stephan Clémencon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We view fairness as a property of distributional stability. Rather than assessing a predictor under a fixed data distribution, we study how its predictions change under perturbations that modify the composition of protected groups. A predictor is fair if it remains stable under such shifts. Under this perspective, several classical notions of fairness arise as stability with respect to specific perturbations, with the associated unfairness gap given by a Lipschitz constant of a prediction-rate functional. This formulation also yields guarantees that hold uniformly over a range of demographic compositions at test time, without requiring knowledge of the deployment distribution. It leads to a learning procedure based on convex combinations of reweighted predictors, formulated as a second-order cone program, for which we establish generalization bounds. Experiments on standard benchmarks illustrate the approach.
  </details>

- **[HyCO: A Hybrid Neural Solver for Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07990)**  `arXiv:2609.07990`  
  _Yuheng Li, Di Yang, Haipeng Chen, Yanhai Xiong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sequential reinforcement learning (RL) solvers and global diffusion model (DM) solvers for neural combinatorial optimization exhibit complementary failure modes under an optimization-regret view. The former enjoys small marginal regret in the early construction stage, but suffers from horizon-wise compounding errors with super-linear regret growth; the latter avoids horizon compounding but incurs linear or sublinear regret w.r.t. the dimension of the remaining unsolved subspace. We propose Hybrid Neural Solver for Combinatorial Optimization (HyCO), a hybrid inference algorithm that constructs a solution prefix with an RL solver and adaptively switches to a conditional DM to complete the remaining decisions. To characterize why such hybridization helps, when to trigger the handover, and how to realize it in practice, we first develop a unified error-scaling theoretical framework and prove that, under explicit error-scaling assumptions, i) the hybrid structure achieves strictly lower expected regret than either backbone alone, and ii) there exists a unique optimal trigger step that minimizes the hybrid regret. We then design a lightweight adaptive trigger that combines policy entropy and RL-DM disagreement to detect trajectory-level signals of the regime shift as a practical proxy, since the optimal trigger step is defined at the expected-regret level and is not directly computable on individual trajectories. Experimental results on diverse benchmarks demonstrate that HyCO achieves consistent improvements over both backbones and support the empirical effectiveness of adaptive triggering. Our code and checkpoints are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ReBRAC-v2: The Return of the King](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01205)**  `arXiv:2608.01205`  
  _Denis Tarasov, Robert K. Katzschmann_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent offline reinforcement learning methods increasingly rely on expressive generative policies and specialized value guidance. We ask how far systematic modernization of a conventional behavior-regularized actor-critic can go while preserving algorithmic simplicity. ReBRAC-v2 directly trains an exact-likelihood normalizing-flow actor with likelihood, MSE, and MAE behavior regularization, classification-based residual critics, staged optimization, and multi-sample inference. We develop one shared configuration through roughly 600 Bayesian proposals on six OGBench tasks, then adapt only two behavior-regularization coefficients using a 16-point grid for the main comparisons. Across ten common OGBench categories, ReBRAC-v2 averages 74.8 versus 52.3 for the next-best method and ranks first in eight categories. Without structural changes, it also obtains the strongest compared averages on D4RL AntMaze (90.2) and Adroit (33.6). Motivated by these results, we extend evaluation to all 235 state-based single-task problems across the 47 dataset variants in the original OGBench suite. To our knowledge, this is the first complete evaluation in this setting; no compared baseline reports results across the full suite. Per-task scores and configurations establish ReBRAC-v2 as a reference baseline for future work beyond the common task subset. The complete cloning objective retains the highest average after coefficient retuning of its alternatives, while further ablations characterize training, capacity, and inference choices. These results demonstrate the strength of systematic engineering within a minimalist offline RL foundation.
  </details>

- **[Bayesian Complete-Pooling in Cross-Subject Classification for Motor Imagery Electroencephalogram](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22980)**  `arXiv:2607.22980`  
  _Ethan Davis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) have long sought calibration-free operation, yet classifiers are typically benchmarked on discrimination alone. Discrimination is blind to calibration, a meaningful gap given that electroencephalogram (EEG) signals are nonstationary and point-estimate classifiers can become overconfident under distribution shift. We conducted a large-scale study contrasting Bayesian complete-pooling models against frequentist baselines for cross-subject, left-hand versus right-hand motor imagery EEG classification across 20 datasets. Each of six frequentist pipelines was paired with an analogous Bayesian pipeline sharing identical, deterministic feature extraction, so that only the classifier was Bayesian. Bayesian classifiers were fit via Markov chain Monte Carlo. Our primary metric was the Brier score, decomposed into reliability and resolution, alongside the area under the ROC curve for discrimination and Shannon entropy for sharpness. For each metric we fit a random-effects meta-analysis with Knapp-Hartung adjustment, verified by leave-one-out influence analysis. Bayesian complete-pooling produced statistically significant improvements in reliability and increases in predictive uncertainty (lower sharpness), with 95% confidence intervals of [-0.0029, -0.0001] for reliability and [0.0022, 0.0220] bits for entropy. Still, neither effect held statistical significance when datasets with flagged posterior sampling were excluded. Brier score, resolution, and discrimination showed no significant differences. Between-study heterogeneity was low across all metrics. Bayesian pipelines consumed roughly thirteen times more energy than their frequentist counterparts, a cost that remains modest relative to common household appliances. This modest cost makes partial-pooling across subjects and sessions, the structure complete-pooling omits, a feasible next step.
  </details>

- **[On the Design Space of Discrete Diffusion Online Adaptation for Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02834)**  `arXiv:2607.02834`  
  _Trevor Chen, Ariel Dai, Jason Yang, Riccardo De Santi, Daniel Khalil, Wenda Chu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular optimization often starts from a pretrained generative model that captures a broad prior over valid molecular structures. At test time, however, the goal is not to sample from this prior, but to use a limited oracle budget to shift generation toward task-specific high-reward molecules. We study this adaptation problem for discrete diffusion models. Each online round couples several choices. The loop must decide which candidates to evaluate, how rewards become model updates, which feedback to reuse, and how far to move beyond the pretrained prior. These choices have mostly been studied in isolation, leaving open whether they complement one another, become redundant, or interfere inside a full online adaptation loop. We conduct controlled studies across six small-molecule binding-affinity tasks and three protein-fitness tasks. We find that acquisition, reward shaping, and model debiasing provide complementary routes to higher reward, especially for small molecules. Replay further stabilizes learning, while validity penalties keep small-molecule exploration on the valid molecular manifold. Together, these findings point to a practical recipe for feedback-efficient molecular optimization: online fine-tuning with acquisition, reward shaping, debiasing, replay, and validity control. This recipe outperforms offline fine-tuning and inference-time search baselines under matched oracle-call budgets and GPU-hour accounting. The gains are largest when high-reward candidates require larger shifts from the pretrained prior.
  </details>

- **[Beyond IID: How General Are Tabular Foundation Models, Really?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30410)**  `arXiv:2606.30410`  
  _Lennart Purucker, Andrej Tschalzev, Nick Erickson, Gioia Blayer, David Holzmüller, Alan Arazi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models for predictive machine learning on tabular data have recently gained significant traction in academia and industry. Research communities across disciplines are increasingly evaluating tabular foundation models on diverse datasets and tasks. However, these task- and discipline-specific evaluations remain largely inaccessible to model researchers because benchmark software and evaluation protocols are fragmented. As a result, model researchers rely on standard benchmarks, which are mostly defined for tasks where tabular foundation models already excel. The most challenging scenarios are excluded, limiting meaningful progress in the field by focusing on marginal improvements on IID data rather than on broader, more demanding challenges. To overcome this, we introduce BeyondArena, the first unified holistic benchmark for tabular data that supports diverse task types (IID, temporal, grouped), across sample size and feature dimensionality scales, with diverse feature types (with text, with high cardinality) from a broad range of disciplines. To enable unified benchmarking beyond standard benchmarks, we introduce DataFoundry, a Python framework and metadata schema for curating tabular datasets for predictive machine learning. Our results across 11 models and 142 curated datasets show that current open-source tabular foundation models are competitive or excel on datasets up to 100k rows, while traditional tree-based and deep learning models still dominate on grouped or large data. BeyondArena guides model research for the most demanding challenges in tabular data, enabling progress towards truly foundational tabular models.
  </details>

- **[MIRAGE: Auditing Anti-Muslim Bias in Frontier LLMs Across Reasoning, Agentic, and Time-Coupled Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16562)**  `arXiv:2606.16562`  
  _Noor Islam S. Mohammad, Tamim Sheikh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Five years after the discovery of persistent anti-Muslim bias in large language models, most evaluations remain confined to single-turn prompt completion, a setting that no longer reflects how frontier LLMs are deployed. We introduce \textbf{MIRAGE} (Muslim-Identity Reasoning and Agentic Generation Evaluation), a benchmark of 1{,}200 prompts spanning three deployment-realistic conditions: direct completion, chain-of-thought reasoning, and simulated agentic decision-making across content moderation, lending triage, refugee claim summarization, and hiring screens. Across six frontier models, we find that (i) chain-of-thought reasoning \emph{amplifies} rather than suppresses Muslim-violence associations by 12--34\% relative to direct completion, (ii) agentic decisions exhibit a 9--22 percentage-point asymmetry between Muslim and matched non-Muslim cases on identical evidence, and (iii) bias is sharply time-coupled to retrieved news context, increasing 18--27\% under recent-conflict retrieval. Existing prompt-based mitigations transfer poorly across our three conditions, suppressing direct-completion bias while leaving agentic asymmetry largely intact. We release MIRAGE and an open evaluation harness to support targeted mitigation research.
  </details>

- **[AME: A Multi-Type Contributor Attribution Framework in Generative AI Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16075)**  `arXiv:2606.16075`  
  _Yang Shi, Songwen Pei, Yang Gao, Bingxue Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative AI enables value creation through multi-stage collaboration among heterogeneous contributors, including training data, base models, fine-tuning behaviors, and prompts. However, how to fairly allocate the data value remains largely unexplored. This paper formulates multi-stage generative AI value allocation as a new research problem and identifies three core challenges: heterogeneous data contribution valuation, data rights mapping, and trustworthy execution. We propose AME (Attribution-Mapping-Execution) framework, a unified framework that integrates data contribution valuation, data rights mapping, and trustworthy execution into a single workflow. Experimental results demonstrate that AME framework achieves data value allocation outcomes more consistent with human reference judgments while maintaining low-cost trustworthy execution. Our work provides an initial foundation for value assessment and revenue allocation in generative AI data markets.
  </details>

- **[How to Score Experts for One-Shot MoE Expert Pruning: A Systematic Formulation and Selection Principle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15716)**  `arXiv:2606.15716`  
  _Zongfang Liu, Jinghui Zhang, Zijian Ma, Guangyi Chen, Xin Yuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models reduce per-token computation through sparse expert activation, yet deployment still requires storing the full expert pool, making one-shot expert pruning a practical approach for reducing memory usage. Although effective, existing criteria are largely heuristic, and no single criterion is universally optimal. Thus, establishing a principle for selecting pruning criteria suited to different deployment objectives remains an important yet largely underexplored problem in one-shot expert pruning. To this end, we introduce a systematic formulation for one-shot MoE expert pruning organized around three factors: routing frequency, gate weighting, and activation strength. The formulation yields a criteria selection principle: task-agnostic pruning should favor routed-token-averaged, gate-free activation-based criteria, whereas task-specific pruning can benefit from retaining routing-frequency and gate-weight information. Beyond this principle, the formulation also provides a systematic view of existing heuristic criteria and gives rise to two new task-agnostic criteria, Mean Activation Norm (MAN) and Mean Squared Activation Norm (MSAN). Across four representative MoE models and 16 diverse benchmarks, MAN and MSAN are consistently strong in the task-agnostic setting, obtain the top-two average ranks, and improve average performance by 4.0 points on average and up to 8.8 points over the strongest baseline.
  </details>

- **[Query Entity-Oriented Graph Neural Networks for Knowledge Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05639)**  `arXiv:2606.05639`  
  _Dongxiao He, Ruqiong Zhang, Zhizhi Yu, Ling Ding, Di Jin, Guangquan Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge Graph Completion (KGC) aims at predicting missing triplets from incomplete knowledge graphs, which is crucial for downstream applications. Recently, Graph Neural Network (GNN)-based methods have achieved remarkable success by performing message passing over a local subgraph centered on the query entity. In these methods, the query relation conditions message computation and aggregation, whereas the query entity serves only as a structural anchor for subgraph extraction. The query relation thus constitutes the sole explicit signal for evidence selection, even though the relevant evidence for the same relation can differ substantially across query entities. We propose Q-GNN, which incorporates the query entity as an explicit conditioning signal. Q-GNN characterizes the query entity by its local structural context and semantic type, and thus remains applicable in the inductive setting. The structural context, encoded by a dedicated encoder, modulates each message through feature-wise linear modulation, while the semantic type guides neighborhood attention and candidate scoring. Experiments in both transductive and inductive settings demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Optimal Transport for Sparse Autoencoder Feature Matching and Circuit Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28567)**  `arXiv:2605.28567`  
  _Tue M. Cao, Nguyen Do, My T. Thai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have become a central tool for interpreting language models. However, two key SAE analyses that remain difficult to scale are (1) matching semantically similar features across multi-layers and (2) compressing large feature circuits into interpretable supernodes. Although these have been treated as separate problems, we show that both are instances of a more fundamental challenge, which we frame as the estimation of semantic distances between SAE features that lie on different activation manifolds. We introduce a distributional framework for this problem, in which each feature is represented not by a single decoder vector like in the literature, but by an activation-weighted distribution over the hidden states that express it. By projecting these distributions into a shared reference space and comparing them with Wasserstein distance, our method provides a unified semantic metric for cross-layer feature comparison.
